# Official visa evidence research

This playbook expands MultiPass Rank’s passport–destination evidence without turning secondary rankings into citations. The current access snapshot is a discovery and consistency-check input; an evidence record is publishable only when an official source supports it.

## Source hierarchy

Prefer sources in this order:

1. Laws, official gazettes, treaties, parliamentary records, and court decisions.
2. The destination’s immigration, border, interior, or foreign ministry and its official application portal.
3. Official embassy or consulate guidance when the central authority does not publish the rule clearly.
4. Intergovernmental treaty repositories, such as EUR-Lex or an official regional-bloc secretariat.
5. Government open-data catalogs and their versioned downloads.

Commercial passport indexes, Timatic summaries, Wikipedia, travel agents, airlines, blogs, and search snippets may help discover a source. They are never evidence citations.

## Evidence record requirements

Every record must identify:

- the destination and affected passport codes;
- the normalized access status;
- an effective date when the source states one, otherwise no invented date;
- expiry or supersession when known;
- material conditions and exceptions;
- at least one direct official URL, its publisher, jurisdiction, language, source type, and review date;
- the official application URL and steps when an ETA or eVisa route exists;
- any conflict with the current MultiPass Rank snapshot.

Store rules at policy level. A regional agreement or nationality list should be one policy with multiple passport codes, not hundreds of duplicated pair records.

## Research pass algorithm

1. Choose one destination, regional agreement, or government dataset. Work in bounded batches.
2. Export the current statuses for affected pairs and use them only as hypotheses.
3. Search the destination’s official domains for visa, exemption, arrival, electronic visa, and travel-authorisation rules in the local language and English.
4. Open the primary document. Confirm scope, status, dates, duration, passport type, and exceptions from the source itself.
5. Follow official links to the current application portal. Do not infer a government relationship from branding alone; verify the domain from an authority page.
6. Add a source record and a policy record. Leave `effectiveFrom` absent if the official source does not state it.
7. Compare the supported status with the current snapshot. Record conflicts for review rather than silently changing either dataset.
8. Run unit, route, sitemap, and structured-data tests. Manually open at least one generated pair per policy.
9. Record the review date. Recheck mutable guidance on a schedule and laws after a reported policy change.
10. Promote only reviewed candidates with `npm run evidence:promote -- <approved-candidate-paths>`. The command appends to the reviewed artifact; use `--replace` only for already-promoted batches and `--from-head` only to recover from the committed artifact.

Good first bulk sources include government nationality lists, official open-data CSV files, and multilateral agreements. Reviewed examples in this repository cover Angola’s 98-nationality tourist exemption, Hong Kong's ordinary-passport visitor schedule and Indian pre-arrival registration, Kenya’s ETA, DR Congo’s official eVisa route, and the EU–Brazil short-stay agreement.

## Pass 2 result: Hong Kong inbound access

The second pass reviewed the Hong Kong Immigration Department's August 2026 visitor schedule at policy level. It added evidence for 144 visa-free ordinary-passport relationships, 48 prior-visa relationships, and India's pre-arrival registration route. The official language describes Indian visitors as remaining visa-free after advance registration, so the upstream `evisa` category is corrected to `eta`/PAR before scoring.

Macao, Mainland China, Taiwan, Timor-Leste, and Kosovo were deliberately not inferred from the ordinary foreign-nationality table. Their special or catch-all treatment is the first queued follow-up batch.

## Pass 3 result: reviewed expansion and developed-destination program

The strong-review pass accepted Kenya's May 2025 legal and operational ETA schedules, the PRC-passport visitor rule for Hong Kong, the HKSAR-passport EU/Schengen exemption, and the reciprocal EU agreements with Colombia and Peru. Kenya's source conflict for Guyana and Indonesia produced two narrow access overrides. Saint Kitts and Nevis remains unsupported until Kenya corrects or clarifies the repeated official spelling `St. Kitts and Navis`.

The HKSAR outbound dataset was useful discovery evidence but was not published for its 29 non-European visa-free or visa-on-arrival claims. Its own caveat directs travellers to destination authorities, so those records remain candidate-only until destination-side confirmation is reviewed. Seven proposed access corrections from that dataset were rejected for the same reason.

The first program targets 42 priority destinations: the EU and Schengen cohort, Ireland, the United Kingdom, the United States, Canada, Australia, New Zealand, Japan, South Korea, Singapore, Hong Kong, Israel, and Taiwan. Run `npm run evidence:coverage` to measure that cohort, or `npm run evidence:coverage -- --all --summary` for the complete matrix and regional totals. The 50% and 80% thresholds are progress indicators, not claims that unsupported relationships are false or that travel advice is complete.

## Pass 4 result: priority-destination baseline

The parallel research and strong-review pass reached 8,139 of 8,316 active current-status relationships (97.9%) across all 42 priority destinations. All 42 have at least 50% coverage and 41 have at least 80%. South Korea remains at 55.6% because the reviewed official sources directly establish its visa-free and K-ETA cohorts but do not safely establish a nationality-wide visa-required complement.

Australia, Canada, Ireland, Japan, New Zealand, Singapore, the United Kingdom, and the United States now have complete catalog baselines. Taiwan has 196 of 198 non-citizenship relationships supported; Mainland China and Palestinian-territory passport treatment remain unresolved. Israel retains one unresolved Palestinian-document relationship. EU and Schengen destinations retain narrow agreement or associated-state gaps rather than borrowing unsupported scope from a neighboring regime.

Temporary programmes are represented with inclusive end dates. Current access overrides for South Korea's temporary K-ETA exemption and Taiwan's trial exemption and eVisa routes stop applying after their official expiry. Emergency or temporary-passport landing-visa routes are documented in research candidates but are not promoted into ordinary-passport canonical rankings or sitemap URLs.

## Pass 5 result: destination-authority regional expansion

The next strong-review tranche promoted destination-side ordinary-passport policies for Brazil, Mexico, Fiji, South Africa, The Bahamas, Mauritius, Malaysia, Barbados, Indonesia, Rwanda, Oman, Jamaica, Zambia, Ecuador, Argentina, and Nepal, plus the reviewed Schengen-associated-state completion. Complete-matrix current-status evidence increased from 9,013 of 44,974 relationships (20.0%) to 11,987 (26.7%). Fifty-eight of 227 destination columns now have at least 50% coverage and 57 have at least 80%. The original 42-destination priority program remains 8,207 of 8,316 (98.7%).

The review changed 195 current pair classifications where destination authorities contradicted or refined the upstream snapshot. Material corrections include Mexico's paid electronic visa for Brazilian passports, Rwanda's universal visa-on-arrival rule, Ecuador's emailed electronic visas, Jamaica's current prior-visa and port-of-entry rows, Indonesia's BVK/VOA split, and narrower corrections for The Bahamas, Mauritius, Malaysia, Barbados, Zambia, and Nepal. Rwanda's fee-waived arrival visas remain `visa_on_arrival`; Indonesia and Nepal online pre-arrival forms remain part of their arrival-visa process; Barbados' online application produces an entry-visa letter rather than an eVisa. Conditional Argentina AVE and Oman exemption routes are documented without replacing the nationality-only default when their third-country-document conditions are absent.

The tranche deliberately retained unresolved cells. Indonesia has 100 passports for which its reviewed official lists do not prove the advance route. Oman has 91 unresolved current-status cells, including a Taiwan divergence between Royal Oman Police and the Foreign Ministry. Mexico retains an unrepresentable Kosovo travel-document restriction; Mauritius retains Montenegro and Kosovo ambiguity; The Bahamas, Barbados, Jamaica, Zambia, and Ecuador retain small omitted or legacy-name groups. The South Korea complement candidate remains unpromoted because absence from the live K-ETA list does not prove a prior-visa rule.

## Pass 6 result: island and Caribbean destination expansion

The next strong-review tranche promoted destination-side ordinary-passport policies for Maldives, Seychelles, Samoa, and a conservative documented subset for Trinidad and Tobago. Complete-matrix current-status evidence increased from 11,987 of 44,974 relationships (26.7%) to 12,651 (28.1%). Sixty-one of 227 destination columns now have at least 50% coverage and 60 have at least 80%. The original 42-destination priority program remains 8,207 of 8,316 (98.7%).

The review changed 51 current pair classifications. Maldives' destination-wide tourist visa is issued on arrival, correcting 11 upstream `visa_free` rows to `visa_on_arrival`. Seychelles requires every foreign visitor to obtain pre-departure electronic travel authorisation, correcting five `visa_free` rows and Kosovo's `visa_required` row to `eta`. Samoa's free Visitor's Permit is issued upon arrival, correcting 28 upstream `visa_free` rows to `visa_on_arrival`. Trinidad and Tobago's current Foreign Ministry instructions and Immigration eVisa service correct China, Taiwan, Cuba, North Korea, Vietnam, and Venezuela to `evisa`.

The tranche deliberately preserves material limitations and official-source disagreements. Maldives prohibits entry on Israeli passports from 15 April 2025, but the current access taxonomy has no `entry_prohibited` status; the cell remains conservatively unverified rather than being misrepresented as an ordinary prior-visa route. Maldives Immigration's current tourist page does not state the initial arrival-visa duration. Seychelles' official sources disagree on the standard authorisation fee (€10 versus €10.90) and application window (30 days versus up to 10 days). Samoa Immigration states a 90-day arrival permit while the Tourism Authority still states less than 60 days. Trinidad and Tobago's official services disagree on the eVisa fee (TTD 800 versus TTD 400), and 127 passport relationships remain unresolved because the reviewed sources do not establish an exhaustive current visa-required complement.

## Pass 7 result: African and Pacific destination expansion

The next strong-review tranche promoted destination-side ordinary-passport policies for Madagascar, Palau, Vanuatu, Cabo Verde, and Mozambique. Complete-matrix current-status evidence increased from 12,651 of 44,974 relationships (28.1%) to 13,562 (30.2%). Sixty-six of 227 destination columns now have at least 50% coverage and 64 have at least 80%. The original 42-destination priority program remains 8,207 of 8,316 (98.7%).

The review changed exactly 300 current pair classifications, with no undeclared snapshot changes. Madagascar's nationality-wide short-stay visa can be issued at an international airport or port, correcting the Palestinian-passport row to `visa_on_arrival`. Palau corrects four Schengen-associated passports to `visa_free`, ten flagged nationalities to mandatory pre-clearance (`eta` in the normalized taxonomy), and Israel to the general `visa_on_arrival` route. Cyprus remains correctly `visa_free` under the reciprocal EU–Palau treaty despite its omission from Palau Immigration's separately worded Schengen list. Vanuatu's official wording calls 113 nationalities “exempt” but expressly says the tourist visa is obtained upon arrival, so those rows are corrected from `visa_free` to `visa_on_arrival`; Cameroon and Mozambique are corrected to `visa_required`. Cabo Verde's current government schedule corrects 19 arrival-visa rows to `visa_free`; EASE pre-registration and the Airport Security Tax remain travel/security formalities rather than an ETA. Mozambique's live National Immigration Service portal corrects 146 arrival-visa rows to `evisa`, São Tomé and Príncipe to `visa_free`, and Ethiopia, Nigeria, and Nepal to `visa_required`.

The tranche deliberately leaves 53 relationships unresolved. Vanuatu's live official nationality page places Bahrain, Monaco, Rwanda, and Türkiye in both its exempt and non-exempt lists, while Kyrgyzstan, Macao, North Macedonia, Moldova, Mongolia, and Palestine appear in neither list. Cabo Verde's two current government schedules omit 42 catalog nationalities; Cuba's only exemption wording is mission-limited and does not prove ordinary visitor treatment. Mozambique's live operational portal expands the Foreign Ministry's separately published eight-country all-passport waiver list; the nine additional portal exemptions are retained with medium confidence and should be rechecked if either authority updates its guidance.

## Pass 8 result: African, Caribbean, and South American expansion

The next strong-review tranche promoted destination-side ordinary-passport policies for Uganda, Benin, Antigua and Barbuda, and Colombia. Complete-matrix current-status evidence increased from 13,562 of 44,974 relationships (30.2%) to 14,169 (31.5%). Sixty-nine of 227 destination columns now have at least 50% coverage and 67 have at least 80%. The original 42-destination priority program remains at 8,207 of 8,316 relationships (98.7%).

The review changed exactly 20 current pair classifications, with no undeclared snapshot changes. Uganda's current 40-nationality exemption schedule and advance online-approval rule correct Angola from prior-visa-required to `evisa`. Antigua and Barbuda's named Immigration Department schedule and electronic-visa default correct Tonga to `visa_free` and twelve omitted or misclassified passports to `evisa`. Colombia's current exemption resolution and normally electronic Visitor-visa process correct Belarus and Mongolia from `visa_free` to `evisa`, and Israel, Malaysia, South Africa, and South Sudan from `visa_required` to `evisa`.

The tranche deliberately rejects stale or over-broad inference. Uganda's still-live 2021 notice contains a different exemption list; the current 40-country schedule controls nationality scope, while the older notice is used only to corroborate advance online processing. Benin's September 2020 flyer claimed a unilateral exemption for every African nationality, but the current passport-type-specific Foreign Ministry register does not reproduce that blanket and limits several former entries to diplomatic or service passports. Only 39 currently named ordinary or national-passport exemptions plus Beninese citizenship are promoted; 159 Benin relationships remain unresolved rather than being guessed from omission. Antigua and Barbuda's live exemption page retains a 2016 publication date and an inaccurate regional heading, so the model follows its explicit country rows and explicit unlisted-country default rather than silently treating all current EU members alike.

## Pass 9 result: Middle East destination expansion

The next strong-review tranche promoted destination-side ordinary-passport policies for Bahrain, Qatar, Saudi Arabia, and Jordan. Complete-matrix current-status evidence increased from 14,169 of 44,974 relationships (31.5%) to 14,496 (32.2%). Seventy of 227 destination columns now have at least 50% coverage and 68 have at least 80%. Middle East coverage increased from 307 of 2,970 relationships (10.3%) to 634 (21.3%), while the original 42-destination priority program remains at 8,207 of 8,316 relationships (98.7%).

The review changed exactly 12 current pair classifications, with no undeclared snapshot changes. Qatar's current passport wizard corrects Serbia to its advance Hayya eVisa route and eight passports to arrival-issued entry. Jordan's restricted-nationality schedule corrects Ghana and Myanmar to prior approval. Saudi Arabia's live country-group and route APIs correct the United Kingdom from ETA to visa on arrival; the same live widget confirms 67 nationality-wide arrival routes, GCC visa-free entry, Russia's operational visa waiver, and five nationalities routed to an embassy or authorized visa office.

The tranche deliberately preserves incomplete and conflicting official guidance. Bahrain's current authorities publish incompatible counts of 68 and 69 visa-on-arrival nationalities without a reconcilable named schedule; only citizenship and the five explicitly named GCC visa-free relationships are supported, leaving 193 cells unresolved. Ukraine's seeded Bahrain `visa_free` status is contradicted by the official online-visa list, but is not replaced because the current sources do not establish whether its correct normalized route is visa on arrival or advance eVisa. Qatar leaves seven passport codes absent from its live selector. Saudi Arabia leaves 120 relationships unresolved, including Brazil because older official eVisa pages and the current widget disagree. Jordan's electronic-services manual proves that a printable eVisa route exists for non-restricted nationalities, but also acknowledges travellers who need no visa without mapping them; 145 cells therefore remain unresolved rather than collapsing visa-free, arrival, and eVisa routes into one category.

## Passes 10–11 result: remaining high-yield Middle East sources

The next reviewed tranche promoted destination-side policies for the United Arab Emirates, Kuwait, Lebanon, Armenia, Iran, and federal Iraq. Complete-matrix current-status evidence increased from 14,496 of 44,974 relationships (32.2%) to 15,101 (33.6%). Seventy-three of 227 destination columns now have at least 50% coverage and 70 have at least 80%. Middle East coverage increased from 634 of 2,970 relationships (21.3%) to 1,239 (41.7%), while the original 42-destination priority program remains at 8,207 of 8,316 relationships (98.7%).

The review changed exactly 71 current pair classifications, with no upstream refresh drift or undeclared changes. The UAE's live Foreign Ministry schedule and digitally issued all-nationalities tourist route produced 37 corrections: 22 named passports now follow the authority's `visa_free` label and 15 prior-visa rows become `evisa`. Armenia's live ordinary-passport tourism checker produced 22 corrections, including Azerbaijan to `visa_free` and 21 old arrival-visa rows to the advance `evisa` route. Kuwait's current Ministry of Interior endpoint corrects Macao to `visa_on_arrival`. Lebanon's General Security rules correct eight passports to border-issued visas and the Dominican Republic, Kazakhstan, and Syria to prior-visa or prior-approval treatment. The reviewed Iran and Iraq policies did not change a seeded status.

This tranche intentionally leaves conflicting and incomplete official data visible. The UAE schedule omits Taiwan. Kuwait's named 53-passport arrival cohort does not establish the other 140 routes, and its GCC entry rule has only medium-confidence official-news reporting rather than a re-openable primary implementing text. Lebanon leaves Egypt, Ghana, and Iraq unresolved because its own provisions overlap or impose gender- or profession-specific rules; Israel remains a taxonomy gap. Armenia's current static invitation-only list conflicts with live eVisa outcomes for 55 ordinary passports, so those cells remain unresolved; its newer residence-permit waiver is documented only as a conditional programme running from 1 July 2026 through 1 July 2027. Iran's current MFA portal supports 27 short tourist exemptions, nine other named waiver relationships, and three arrival permits but no exhaustive complement, leaving 159 cells unresolved. Iraq's federal eVisa portal advertises electronic entry visas while its public nationality API currently returns no countries; only Iraqi citizenship is therefore supported, with 198 relationships unresolved and Kurdistan Region rules kept out of the federal column.

## Pass 12 result: Middle East authority and territory gaps

The final Middle East destination pass reviewed the Palestinian territories, Syria, and Yemen. Complete-matrix current-status evidence increased from 15,101 of 44,974 relationships (33.6%) to 15,297 (34.0%). Seventy-four of 227 destination columns now have at least 50% coverage and 71 have at least 80%. Middle East coverage increased from 1,239 of 2,970 relationships (41.7%) to 1,435 (48.3%), while the original 42-destination priority program remains at 8,207 of 8,316 relationships (98.7%).

Yemen's current Aden-based Immigration, Passports and Nationality Authority grid supports an advance Visit Visa route for 196 catalog passports. Although applications start in the electronic portal, the authority requires prior approval and a final embassy or consulate visa sticker, so these relationships are `visa_required`, not eVisa or visa on arrival. This corrects exactly 12 seeded relationships: eleven former `visa_on_arrival` rows and Hong Kong's former `visa_free` row. Israel, Kosovo, and Yemeni citizenship remain unresolved because the reviewed official sources do not directly establish those classifications.

The Palestinian-territory and Syrian columns remain entirely unverified after review. Current Israeli and COGAT sources describe individual crossings, territorial coordination, and foreign-visitor permits but no nationality-wide Palestinian-destination matrix; Israeli destination rules were not copied into that column. Syria's current civil-aviation publication says travellers need an advance visa unless exempt, but publishes no current exemption schedule. Strong review also removed initially proposed same-country citizenship cells for Syria and Yemen because their foreign-visitor sources did not directly establish citizen entry rights. These red cells represent documented source and taxonomy gaps rather than an assumption that the seeded statuses are false.

## Pass 13 result: South and Southeast Asia expansion

The next reviewed tranche promoted destination-side ordinary-passport policies for India, Sri Lanka, and Vietnam. Complete-matrix current-status evidence increased from 15,297 of 44,974 relationships (34.0%) to 15,888 (35.3%). Seventy-seven of 227 destination columns now have at least 50% coverage and 74 have at least 80%. Asia coverage is 2,204 of 6,336 relationships (34.8%), while the original 42-destination priority program remains at 8,207 of 8,316 relationships (98.7%).

The pass changed exactly 16 seeded classifications, with no upstream refresh drift or undeclared changes. India's current eVisa list and advance-visa framework correct Afghanistan, China, Hong Kong, Macao, Saudi Arabia, Timor-Leste, and Uganda from `evisa` to `visa_required`. Sri Lanka's current Department programme requires an ETA even when its fee is waived, correcting Maldives, Seychelles, and Singapore from `visa_free`, India from `visa_on_arrival`, and Cameroon, Côte d'Ivoire, Ghana, Nigeria, and North Korea from `visa_required` to `eta`. Vietnam's current legal all-foreigners eVisa scope and 39 ordinary-passport exemptions already matched every seeded classification.

India and Vietnam now have a supported classification for all 199 catalog passports. Sri Lanka has 196 supported cells and retains Taiwan, Kosovo, and Syria as unresolved because its current nationality alert and broader ETA/airport guidance do not reconcile those passport codes safely. Other material conditions remain policy-level rather than nationality-wide shortcuts: India's Nepal and Bhutan exemptions are route-limited, UAE arrival eligibility is conditioned on prior Indian visa history, and Pakistan's ordinary visitor processing is currently suspended; Sri Lanka's five-nationality sponsor process is still an advance ETA; Vietnam's eVisa is limited to designated gates and does not absorb overseas-Vietnamese, border-pass, residence, diplomatic, official, or special-document routes.

## Pass 14 result: Thailand current-scheme completion

Thailand's reviewed destination column adds current official support for all 199 catalog passports, bringing complete-matrix current-status evidence to 16,086 of 44,974 relationships (35.8%). Seventy-eight of 227 destination columns now have at least 50% coverage and 75 have at least 80%. Asia coverage is 2,402 of 6,336 relationships (37.9%). The easiest-access partition is Thai citizenship, 97 visa-free ordinary passports, 15 visa-on-arrival passports, and 86 advance eVisa passports.

The review corrects exactly two seeded classifications: Ethiopia and Vanuatu are in the current 15-day visa-on-arrival schedule, not the visa-exemption cohort. Thailand's Digital Arrival Card remains a separate mandatory arrival declaration for non-Thai travellers and is not normalized as an ETA or visa. The Cabinet approved a replacement scheme on 19 May 2026, but the official notice says it will take effect only 15 days after the relevant Ministry of Interior notices are published in the Royal Gazette. No later operative notice was found during this review, and the Consular Affairs Department still links the current 60-day and visa-on-arrival schedules; because the transition may happen quickly, those policies require an immediate recheck when a Gazette notice appears.

## Pass 15 result: mainland Southeast Asia and Philippines expansion

The next strong-review tranche promoted destination-side ordinary-passport policies for the Philippines, Cambodia, and Laos. Complete-matrix current-status evidence increased from 16,086 of 44,974 relationships (35.8%) to 16,458 (36.6%). Eighty of 227 destination columns now have at least 50% coverage and 77 have at least 80%. Asia coverage increased from 2,402 of 6,336 relationships (37.9%) to 2,774 (43.8%).

The pass changed exactly 147 seeded classifications, with no upstream refresh drift or undeclared changes. The Philippines' current Foreign Affairs schedule corrects Tuvalu from visa on arrival to `visa_free`; the renewed Chinese Taipei waiver remains `visa_free` from 1 July 2026 through 30 June 2027. Laos' current Foreign Affairs exemption table corrects Belarus and North Korea from visa on arrival to `visa_free`. Its live official tourist-eVisa selector corrects 142 former visa-on-arrival rows, the Democratic Republic of the Congo's prior-visa row, and Hong Kong's visa-free row to advance `evisa`.

The Philippines now has current official support for every catalog passport: Philippine citizenship, 162 visa-free ordinary passports, and 36 advance-visa passports. Its authorities disagree on whether Brunei and Cambodia receive 30 or 21 days, and China's temporary 14-day programme expires on 15 January 2027. Cambodia remains deliberately narrow: nine named ASEAN passports and a temporary CN/HK/MO exemption are supported, while 187 relationships stay unresolved because the all-countries announcement concerns ordinary/business Visa E and no exhaustive tourist Visa T or arrival-visa nationality matrix could be reopened. Cambodia e-Arrival is an immigration, health, and customs declaration, not an ETA.

Laos supports 18 visa-free and 144 eVisa relationships; 37 remain unresolved, including Lao citizenship because the reviewed foreigner-management material does not directly establish a citizen ordinary-passport entry rule. The eVisa selector is operational evidence but has stale exemption metadata, and Immigration's generic arrival-visa material publishes no nationality list. India and Nepal remain on the same eVisa route despite a selector watchlist acknowledgement, which is preserved as an extra-review condition rather than normalized into another status.

## Pass 16 result: Mongolia and Uzbekistan expansion

The next strong-review tranche promoted destination-side ordinary-passport policies for Mongolia and Uzbekistan. ## Pass 18 result: Macao, Brunei, and Kyrgyzstan expansion

The next strong-review tranche promoted destination-side ordinary-passport policies for Macao SAR, Brunei Darussalam, and Kyrgyzstan. Complete-matrix current-status evidence increased from 17,110 of 44,974 relationships (38.0%) to 17,579 (39.1%). Eighty-six of 227 destination columns now have at least 50% coverage and 82 have at least 80%. Asia coverage increased from 3,426 of 6,336 relationships (54.1%) to 3,895 (61.5%).

The pass changed exactly four seeded classifications, with no upstream refresh drift or undeclared changes. Macao's current Public Security Police schedule corrects Argentina and Fiji from visa on arrival to `visa_free`, and the Dominican Republic and Taiwan from `visa_free` to checkpoint-issued `visa_on_arrival`. The Dominican Republic is intentionally distinct from Dominica, which remains in Macao's 90-day exemption cohort. Brunei and Kyrgyzstan's reviewed classifications already matched every supported seeded row.

Macao supports 196 relationships: 88 visa-free passports, 102 checkpoint-issued Entry Permits expressly described as visas upon arrival, and six advance-visa passports. Mainland China, Hong Kong, and Macao remain unresolved because the official rules for those travellers are document-, status-, and transit-specific rather than ordinary-passport nationality rules. The source is a current PSP-maintained government service page, but it summarizes Chief Executive written instructions rather than reproducing every underlying instrument.

Brunei supports all 198 foreign-passport relationships: 59 visa-free, five visa on arrival, and 134 advance-visa passports. Its central Foreign Affairs country-by-country category schedule provides an explicit advance-visa complement; it takes precedence over older official mission summaries with different durations. The 30-day D2 waiver requires air entry through Brunei International Airport and confirmed onward travel, while D3 remains a conditional 14-day waiver. E-Arrival is an immigration declaration, not an ETA or visa. Bruneian citizenship remains unresolved without a directly applicable right-of-entry source.

Kyrgyzstan supports 75 visa-free relationships and leaves 124 unresolved. Its current government lists directly establish the exemption cohorts and durations, including Hong Kong and Macao permanent residents and Maldivian citizens for 30 days. The live eVisa service is document-specific and its country-result endpoint became unavailable during both research and independent review, so no eVisa or prior-visa complement was inferred. Kyrgyz citizenship is likewise unresolved because the cited visitor sources do not directly establish citizen entry.

## Pass 19 result: mainland China baseline completion

The next strong-review pass promoted mainland China's current ordinary-passport visitor baseline. Complete-matrix current-status evidence increased from 17,579 of 44,974 relationships (39.1%) to 17,774 (39.5%). Eighty-seven of 227 destination columns now have at least 50% coverage and 83 have at least 80%. Asia coverage increased from 3,895 of 6,336 relationships (61.5%) to 4,090 (64.6%).

The pass changed exactly two seeded classifications, with no upstream refresh drift or undeclared changes. China's current Foreign Affairs register corrects Samoa from `visa_required` to `visa_free` under the mutual ordinary-passport agreement effective 2 April 2025. It corrects Dominica from `visa_free` to `visa_required`: the register limits that waiver to an “ordinary passport for public affairs,” so the statutory advance-visa baseline applies to a general ordinary passport.

China supports 195 relationships: 28 bilateral or permanent ordinary-passport exemptions, 48 temporary unilateral exemptions through 31 December 2026, Brunei's separately listed unilateral exemption, Russia's unilateral exemption through 31 December 2027, and 117 advance-visa passports under the national exit-entry framework. Mainland Chinese, Hong Kong, Macao, and Taiwan document holders remain unresolved because their entry rights depend on distinct right-of-return, Home Return Permit, or Taiwan Compatriot documentation rather than the modeled foreign ordinary-passport rule. Transit, Hainan, tour-group, port-visa, residence, and special-document programmes remain conditions or separate routes and are not normalized as nationality-wide visitor access.

## Pass 20 result: Bhutan, Bangladesh, and Pakistan expansion

The next strong-review tranche promoted destination-side ordinary-passport policies for Bhutan, Bangladesh, and Pakistan. Complete-matrix current-status evidence increased from 17,774 of 44,974 relationships (39.5%) to 18,181 (40.4%). Eighty-nine of 227 destination columns now have at least 50% coverage and 85 have at least 80%. Asia coverage increased from 4,090 of 6,336 relationships (64.6%) to 4,497 (71.0%), while the original 42-destination priority program remains at 8,207 of 8,316 relationships (98.7%).

The pass changed exactly 66 seeded classifications, with no upstream refresh drift or undeclared changes. Bhutan's current Immigration entry page corrects India's `visa_free` row to an arrival-issued entry permit, normalized as `visa_on_arrival`. Bangladesh's current Foreign Affairs guidance corrects Egypt and Brunei from `evisa` to `visa_on_arrival`; its online MRV workflow still requires the completed form to be printed and submitted to a mission. Pakistan's named tourist Visa Prior to Arrival cohort corrects 47 rows to `eta` because travelers must obtain electronic authorization 48–72 hours before travel. Qatar alone has a direct ordinary-passport arrival visa in that schedule, and 15 List A passports are corrected from `evisa` to `visa_required` because they use the advance mission route.

Bhutan now has evidence for all 199 catalog pairs: Bhutanese citizenship, Indian entry permit on arrival, Bangladeshi and Maldivian visas on arrival, and 195 advance eVisas. The current Immigration page is exhaustive for these visitor routes, while diplomatic or official Thai and Swiss passports, SAARC exemption stickers, tour arrangements, and the Sustainable Development Fee remain policy conditions. Official pages describe the fee as a USD 200 base with a temporary USD 100 rate or simply the current USD 100 rate; the classification does not depend on resolving that presentation difference.

Pakistan supports 190 relationships: two visa-free passports, Qatar's true visa on arrival, 47 ETA/VPA passports, 125 eVisa passports, and 15 advance-visa passports. Afghanistan, Armenia, Hong Kong, Israel, India, Macao, Pakistan, Somalia, and Taiwan remain unresolved. A newer Foreign Affairs announcement says the VPA program covers 120 countries but publishes no nationality list, while the detailed Immigration schedule names 50 tourist nationalities and was last modified in March 2024. The model follows only the named schedule and records the larger unnamed claim as a recheck risk.

Bangladesh deliberately remains narrow: Bangladeshi citizenship and 19 expressly named visa-on-arrival passports are supported; 179 cells remain unresolved. Mission guidance also uses phrases such as “all European countries” and describes reciprocity, third-country-departure, investor, sponsor, and security-clearance routes that cannot be converted into an unconditional passport-code schedule. No advance-visa or eVisa complement was inferred, and No Visa Required endorsements for people of Bangladeshi origin remain document- and family-conditioned rather than nationality-wide access.

## Pass 21 result: Tajikistan and documented Central Asian source gaps

The next strong-review tranche promoted Tajikistan's complete ordinary-passport visitor schedule and conservative evidence records for Turkmenistan and Afghanistan. Complete-matrix current-status evidence increased from 18,181 of 44,974 relationships (40.4%) to 18,379 (40.9%). Ninety of 227 destination columns now have at least 50% coverage and 86 have at least 80%. Asia coverage increased from 4,497 of 6,336 relationships (71.0%) to 4,695 (74.1%).

Tajikistan changed exactly 73 seeded classifications, with no upstream refresh drift or undeclared changes. Its current Foreign Affairs schedules and live electronic-visa selector support Tajik citizenship, 101 visa-free passports, 41 advance eVisas, and 56 advance consular visas. Fifteen former eVisa or arrival-visa rows are now `visa_free`, five former arrival-visa rows are `evisa`, and 53 former eVisa or arrival-visa rows use the explicit `visa_required` residual. The live selector contains 130 records, while the written simplified/eVisa list does not map one-to-one; the model gives a documented visa-free route precedence, uses the live selector for eVisa-only passports, and applies the written schedule's express all-other-countries consular default. The SIP Foreign Affairs PDF and the constitutional source were directly recoverable only with TLS-certificate validation bypass, so that provenance risk remains recorded for recheck.

Turkmenistan supports only its same-country citizenship relationship. Its law directly gives citizens a right to enter, while the foreign-entry visa default is subject to international treaties for which no exhaustive current schedule was found. A 2025 amendment genuinely creates an electronic-visa mechanism and removes invitations for eVisa applications, but delegates the eligible categories and procedures to a separate Presidential act that was not published or recoverable. Airport visa fees likewise do not establish nationality eligibility. All 198 foreign cells remain unresolved.

Afghanistan remains entirely unresolved, including Afghan citizenship. Current Foreign Affairs pages describe tourist, visitor, and approval-conditioned Kabul Airport visa categories, and the Interior Ministry exposes a visa form, but none publishes a nationality schedule, exemption list, eVisa scope, exhaustive ordinary-passport default, or direct right-of-entry rule. Independent review also corrected an initial retrieval diagnosis that had treated the Foreign Affairs domain as compromised: the live domain is current official ministry content, but still insufficient for passport-code normalization.

## Pass 22 result: Timor-Leste and Asian complement review

The next strong-review tranche promoted Timor-Leste's complete visitor scope, 90 newly supported Indonesian eVisa relationships, and a documented North Korea source gap. Complete-matrix current-status evidence increased from 18,379 of 44,974 relationships (40.9%) to 18,641 (41.4%). Ninety-two of 227 destination columns now have at least 50% coverage and 88 have at least 80%. Asia coverage increased from 4,695 of 6,336 relationships (74.1%) to 4,957 (78.2%).

Timor-Leste changed exactly nine seeded classifications, with no upstream refresh drift or undeclared changes. Its current Customs guidance supports the United States tourism exemption and route-limited visa on arrival for the residual foreign cohort, while the binding reciprocal EU–Timor-Leste agreement supports 26 EU ordinary passports and has applied regardless of transport mode since 1 May 2022. Eight non-EU rows previously labeled `visa_free` are corrected to `visa_on_arrival`, while the United States changes from arrival visa to `visa_free`. The final partition is 27 visa-free passports, 171 arrival-visa passports, and Timorese citizenship. The general arrival route is available only at Dili International Airport and Dili Sea Port; Indonesian passports additionally have an all-border arrival route, and other land arrivals need advance Visa Application Authorization. The 2017 law supports the 30-day stay, one equal extension, Oe-Cusse travel exception, and prohibition on professional activity. Customs omits the EU treaty cohort, but the current treaty expressly controls its scoped short stays and no suspension was found; both sources should be rechecked together for future changes.

Indonesia now supports 189 catalog relationships: the previously promoted citizenship, visa-free and arrival-visa cohorts plus 90 additional C1 electronic tourist-visa passports. Ten remain unresolved: Afghanistan, Cameroon, Guinea, Israel, North Korea, Liberia, Niger, Nigeria, Somalia, and Kosovo. Two current official regional Immigration schedules disagree on the Calling Visa list: Bontang names six countries, while Sulawesi Tenggara adds Cameroon, Guinea, and Niger. Those disputed passports remain unresolved, as does Kosovo because the live selector did not disclose its mapping. Central C1 guidance and the official portal support account-based electronic application and downloadable issuance, but a regional C1 page retains sponsor-template instructions; this operational disagreement is recorded rather than flattened.

North Korea remains entirely unresolved. Its current Warsaw embassy page and entry-visa form confirm advance consular applications, ordinary-passport and nationality fields, invitation/contact data, ports, fees, and refusal discretion, but publish no nationality eligibility, exemption, default, or citizen right-of-return rule. Central DPRK web sources were inaccessible or had TLS failures during review, so the seeded 198 prior-visa rows and same-country citizenship row were not converted into verified claims.

The South Korea candidate was also revalidated against the current K-ETA table and temporary exemption extension. Its 24 temporary visa-free, 86 K-ETA, and citizenship policies already match the canonical evidence and current fallback, so no duplicate reviewed policy batch was promoted. Eighty-eight cells remain unresolved because the visa portal's status-limited electronic services do not prove a nationality-wide advance-visa complement. The temporary K-ETA exemptions require recheck after 31 December 2026.

## Pass 23 result: Cambodia named prior-visa correction

The next focused review added ten directly named Cambodian prior-visa relationships, increasing complete-matrix current-status evidence from 18,641 of 44,974 relationships (41.4%) to 18,651 (41.5%) and Asia coverage from 4,957 of 6,336 (78.2%) to 4,967 (78.4%). Afghanistan, Bangladesh, Algeria, Iran, Iraq, Nigeria, Pakistan, Saudi Arabia, Sudan, and Sri Lanka change from the seeded `visa_on_arrival` status to `visa_required` because Cambodia's current official Paris mission guidance says these passports must obtain a visa before departure.

Strong review rejected a much broader initial interpretation of the same mission page. Its generic arrival-visa procedure does not expressly create an all-other-nationalities Tourist Visa T entitlement, and the central Tourist Visa and eVisa pages were trapped in redirect loops during both research and review. The 176 unlisted foreign passports therefore remain unresolved, as does Cambodian citizenship; e-Arrival remains an immigration, health, and customs declaration rather than a visa route. The named ten are the only defensible addition until a current central exhaustive schedule or working selector can be reopened.

## Pass 24 result: Botswana and conservative Asian complement closure

The next strong-review tranche promoted Botswana's bounded official schedules, two live-selector Kyrgyz exemptions, and conservative residual records for Myanmar and Laos. Complete-matrix current-status evidence increased from 18,651 of 44,974 relationships (41.5%) to 18,823 (41.9%). Ninety-three of 227 destination columns now have at least 50% coverage and 89 have at least 80%. Africa coverage increased from 2,255 of 11,289 relationships (20.0%) to 2,425 (21.5%); Asia added two relationships and remains 78.4% after rounding.

Botswana supports 170 relationships: 79 visa-free passports and 91 advance electronic visas. Rwanda changes from `visa_free` to `evisa`, and Taiwan changes from `visa_required` to `evisa`; online approval must be obtained before travel, so printing the approved visa at the port is not a visa on arrival. Twenty-nine cells remain unresolved. The Washington embassy's stated exemption schedule conflicts with the current Tourism Organisation required-visa list for 23 passports, the same embassy page is internally inconsistent for the Holy See/Vatican, four catalog codes are omitted, and no direct citizenship entry rule was found. Those rows are not forced into either schedule. The central Government application page is authoritative for electronic processing, while the Tourism Organisation and bare eVisa frontend have older or unreproducible publication signals.

Kyrgyzstan's session-bound official selector directly returned “Visa free regime” for Albanian and Thai ordinary passports, both for up to 30 days. Those two cells already matched the fallback, so they add evidence without changing rankings; 122 cells remain unresolved. The endpoint requires a session cookie and POST country identifier and is mutable, so facilitated-regime results were not reinterpreted as tourist eVisa eligibility.

Myanmar and Laos gained no new supported pairs. Myanmar's current named tourist-VOA passports are already covered by promoted eVisa or easier routes, while its generic VOA and embassy wording is not exhaustive; 96 remain unresolved. Laos' live eVisa API still returns the same 162 positive records, while Immigration's port-level VOA labels and the decree's unavailable referenced nationality notice cannot establish treatment for the 37 residual cells. Neither country's same-country citizenship cell was inferred from foreign visitor material.

## Pass 31 result: Sudan and South Sudan

Foreign Affairs distinguishes visa, arrival and mission routes but does not map them by nationality; the live Interior eVisa portal proves electronic issuance without publishing eligibility, and the Government Trade Hub's general arrival wording is non-exhaustive. The Foreign Affairs table has an expired TLS certificate, a 2024 modification date, and a malformed Antigua row, so the narrow four-code cohort is retained at medium confidence and requires periodic recheck.

## Pass 33 result: Republic of the Congo and Gabon

This narrow CEMAC pass increased complete-matrix evidence from 21,789 to 21,799 of 44,974 relationships (48.5%); Africa coverage increased from 5,391 to 5,401 of 11,289 relationships (47.8%).

Republic of the Congo and Gabon each support only the five other CEMAC ordinary passports as visa-free. Current CEMAC material confirms that member-state citizen free movement is effective, while the archived common convention supplies valid-document and three-month visitor conditions that current regional guidance does not independently restate. Both same-country cells remain unresolved.

Congo's foreigner law preserves unspecified particular immigration agreements, so its general valid-visa rule cannot safely establish an all-other-passports advance-visa residual. Its only official eVisa material is a 2019 project presentation, not an operational service. Gabon's current Government eVisa notice confirms an air-only service through Libreville but publishes no nationality eligibility/default; its Foreign Affairs exemption register is dated 2011. Neither source set was stretched into a residual eVisa, visa-on-arrival, or advance-visa claim.

A separately reviewed Chad candidate was not promoted in this pass. Its 2025 decree is discoverable in current Official Journal indexing and appears to preserve CEMAC access plus unnamed bilateral exemptions, but the primary Journal URL could not be reopened during independent review. ## Pass 34 result: Central African Republic and Equatorial Guinea

This second narrow CEMAC pass increased complete-matrix evidence from 21,799 to 21,809 of 44,974 relationships (48.5%); Africa coverage increased from 5,401 to 5,411 of 11,289 relationships (47.9%).

The Central African Republic and Equatorial Guinea each support only the five other CEMAC ordinary passports as visa-free. The Central African Republic's current Paris mission “online” short-stay form must still be printed and sent with the passport and documents, so it is a consular workflow rather than evidence of eVisa issuance. No current central nationality schedule or residual route was found, and its same-country cell remains unresolved.

Equatorial Guinea changes five seeded CEMAC rows from eVisa to visa-free. Independent review caught a material failure in the initial candidate: every cited path on the former Equatorial Guinea eVisa host now serves a July 2026 Amazon/VFS application with United Arab Emirates metadata and content. The entire proposed 193-passport eVisa residual was removed. The Government's 2023 launch announcement remains historical evidence only, and a 2024 Barbados waiver announcement says further procedures were still required. All non-CEMAC passports and Equatorial Guinea's same-country cell remain unresolved.

Djibouti was also independently reviewed but adds no supported cells. Its current official sources prove that a genuine advance eVisa service exists, yet publish neither passport-nationality eligibility nor a complete exemption/default schedule; the entry law preserves unnamed treaty and special regimes. The all-unresolved candidate is retained for source-gap tracking rather than promoted as an invented complement.

Comoros likewise remains entirely unresolved. Its current Justice Ministry publication of the 1988 entry law reserves duly published conventions and special laws, while the only airport/port issuance it expressly describes is a five-day transit visa rather than ordinary tourist entry. No current official convention register, visitor-arrival schedule, eVisa service, or same-country return rule was recovered, so the reviewed source-gap candidate is retained without promotion.

## Pass 36 result: São Tomé and Príncipe

Strong review removed a material overstatement before promotion. The initial candidate treated 138 passports as visa-free under Article 25(1)(f), but that exception is cumulative: the traveler must hold a valid passport, also hold a valid Schengen or United States visa, and stay no more than 15 days. It is a third-country-document-conditioned route, not a nationality baseline, so all 138 rows were moved back to unresolved and 134 proposed fallback changes were discarded.

The retained cohort combines 36 statutory CPLP, EU, United States, and Canadian exemptions with 20 currently named Foreign Ministry waiver partners. The current Ministry brief does not provide agreement-by-agreement duration, passport-type, purpose, or effective-date details, so the additional cohort remains medium confidence. São Tomé citizenship and all unsupported passports remain unresolved.

The 172 affected rows are therefore normalized as ETA—prior travel authorization followed by arrival issuance—rather than eVisa or walk-up visa on arrival. All 172 resulting fallback changes were mechanically matched to the reviewed candidate. The selector is mutable and should be rechecked before travel or future data refreshes.

Benin's Immigration Directorate expressly assigns foreign visitors outside ECOWAS or without such a waiver to the online tourist/visit eVisa and requires them to hold it before arrival. The official workflow confirms electronic application, payment and pre-travel issuance. Together these sources support a complete split of 39 visa-free, 159 eVisa and one citizenship row. Burkina Faso, Mali and Niger remain exempt through current UEMOA entries in Benin's own register rather than through an inferred post-ECOWAS rule.

This complete partition corrects 30 fallback labels from `visa_free` to `evisa`: Algeria, Angola, Botswana, Cameroon, China, Comoros, the Democratic Republic of the Congo, Djibouti, Egypt, Equatorial Guinea, Eritrea, Ethiopia, Gabon, Lesotho, Libya, Madagascar, Malawi, Mauritania, Morocco, Mozambique, Namibia, Somalia, South Sudan, Sudan, Eswatini, Tanzania, Uganda, the United Arab Emirates, Zambia and Zimbabwe. The other 129 residual eVisa labels match current official evidence. The dynamic Ministry API is intermittently slow, so its exact endpoints and 21 August review date are retained for periodic recheck.

Côte d'Ivoire adds no new canonical row beyond its existing 14 ECOWAS and AES-transition exemptions. Its State-recognised SNEDAI workflow is online pre-enrolment and authority approval followed by mandatory biometrics and printed visa issuance at Abidjan airport. It is therefore neither a genuinely electronically issued visa nor an unconditional walk-up visa on arrival, and the current sources publish no nationality eligibility or residual rule. The other 185 rows, including Ivorian citizenship, remain unresolved; Guinea's visa-free correction was already present in the current fallback.

The Gambia likewise adds no duplicate policy beyond its existing citizenship and 32 reconciled visa-free rows. Its current Immigration table is internally inconsistent for Indonesia, Brunei and Eswatini, contains unsafe territory labels, and does not say whether nationality-specific entry clearance leads to a mission or airport visa. Generic sticker/endorsement and port-issuance guidance cannot establish an eVisa, ETA, visa-on-arrival or advance-visa residual. The remaining 166 rows stay visibly unverified, and a proposed Russia conflict was removed because the current fallback already correctly says visa-free.

A future `entry_unavailable` or equivalent status is required before these findings can safely change the public classification.

Canonical evidence contains 344 reviewed batches, 1,723 official sources and 1,185 policies.

The audits cover Canada, Spain, Italy, Belgium, Austria, Portugal, Greece, Switzerland, Denmark, Sweden, Finland, Norway, Poland, Czechia, Hungary, Romania, Slovakia, Lithuania, Bulgaria, Slovenia, Latvia, Croatia, Estonia, Cyprus, Malta, Luxembourg, Iceland, Liechtenstein, Australia, New Zealand, Japan, South Korea, Singapore and Israel. Current passport-government guidance was deliberately used where destination authorities publish only a portal or an incomplete positive list. Matching fallback rows gain evidence without changing their public status, while an override is applied only where the official guidance also establishes actual issuance timing.

Material corrections include Canadian access to Djibouti as `evisa` and Paraguay as `visa_free`; Spanish access to South Sudan, Bangladesh and Guinea-Bissau as `visa_required`; Italian access to Chad as `evisa`, Syria and Burkina Faso as `visa_on_arrival`, Guinea-Bissau as `visa_required` and Djibouti as `evisa`; and Belgian access to Guinea-Bissau as `visa_required`. Austria's Iraq route is `visa_required`, Denmark's Sierra Leone route is `evisa`, Norway's Iran route is `visa_required`, and several other northern and central European rows correct their fallback issuance timing. Côte d'Ivoire remains `visa_on_arrival` where online pre-enrolment ends with airport sticker issuance.

Iraq's current Foreign Ministry guidance produces a second important correction cohort. Swiss, Lithuanian, Croatian, Estonian, Latvian, Maltese and Japanese ordinary passports receive the federal visa at a land, air or sea border, so their fallback `evisa` labels become `visa_on_arrival`; electronic issuance remains an alternative rather than the controlling easiest timing. By contrast, current Austrian and Slovak guidance requires advance mission issuance and those two rows become `visa_required`. Australian guidance corrects Paraguay to `visa_free`, Iran to `visa_required` and Trinidad and Tobago to `evisa`; New Zealand's Paraguay tourist waiver is `visa_free` through 13 August 2027.

Strong review rejected several plausible-looking claims. Ukraine's still-live nationality table labels itself “as of June 08, 2023”, so it cannot establish 2026 currentness for Malta, Luxembourg, Iceland or Israel. Latvia's apparent Turkmenistan airport route requires a State Migration Service-certified invitation or letter of invitation and is therefore not an unconditional passport-only visa on arrival. An Israeli crossing page likewise says Jordan issues the visa at the terminal but requires advance coordination through travel agents, a hotel booking and an authorized guide, so that pair remains unresolved. Chad's self-authenticated portal and Sierra Leone's internally contradictory eVisa/airport-issuance material remain held except where a current passport-government page independently resolves a particular pair. Legal travel prohibitions, destination non-recognition and unavailable routes for Israeli passports are not mislabeled as ordinary advance visas.

Kyrgyzstan and federal Iraq are `evisa`; Jordan is `visa_on_arrival`; Tunisia is `visa_free` for tourists under its stated hotel and return-ticket conditions; Peru, Panama, the Marshall Islands and North Macedonia require advance visas; and Comoros issues a biometric visa at Moroni airport. Thailand's Foreign Ministry schedule is dated 4 August 2026, separates ordinary from diplomatic or official passports, and adds Panama visa-free plus arrival visas for Cabo Verde, Comoros, Guinea-Bissau, Jordan and Sierra Leone. Independent review noted the schedule's general instruction to recheck destination authorities and the unresolved destination-side Sierra Leone issuance conflict. The rows remain promoted under the established rule that a current passport-government schedule can resolve a named pair—the same rule used for other current outbound guidance—and Panama is additionally corroborated from its destination side. These routes should receive an early recheck if Thailand replaces the schedule or a destination publishes a newer controlling nationality map.

Strong review left 109 scoped pairs unresolved. It rejected omission-based complements, portal-only eligibility, conditional residence or third-country-document routes, invitation or LOI processing, special-passport treaties, a Ukrainian table expressly dated June 2023, Grenada's February 2021 schedule, and operational restrictions that do not map to the six access statuses. Morocco's other 32 scoped rows produced no second direct current official status; Brunei leaves 28, China 23 and Thailand 26 unresolved.

## Held from promotion: Djibouti, Sierra Leone, Trinidad and Tobago, Iran and Iraq

Djibouti's current official material proves that an approved eVisa is emailed before travel and replaced point-of-entry visas, but supplies no current nationality eligibility or exemption schedule; all 184 residual rows remain unresolved. Sierra Leone's authenticated portal still says both that an accepted eVisa is emailed and that the visa is issued at the airport or border, so its 184 residual rows cannot be normalized safely; the National Tourist Board's undated broad arrival statement also conflicts with its dated 2019 narrow schedule. Trinidad and Tobago's current positive waiver list does not establish the status of omitted nationalities, and its live selector could not be reproduced through trusted retrieval; all 124 residual rows remain held after a fresh official-source recheck.

Iran's two current MFA eVisa endpoints remain trapped in self-redirecting HTTP 307 loops, leaving all 158 residual rows unresolved, including the special United States, United Kingdom and Canadian treatment and the unrepresentable Israel-related restriction. Iraq's current Interior portal proves pre-travel emailed eVisa issuance, but its nationality selector API returns an empty list; all 182 residual federal-Iraq rows remain unverified without inferring a complement or importing Kurdistan-region rules.

Burkina Faso's authenticated service confirms genuine pre-travel electronic issuance but gives no current ordinary-passport eligibility or exemption scope, so all 144 residual rows stay unresolved. Chad's eVisa service describes automatic pre-entry issuance, but it remains self-authenticated while Foreign Affairs is in maintenance and Interior returns 403; all 171 residual rows remain held. Kuwait's live MOI arrival API still confirms only the already supported 53-passport cohort. A separate current MOI instruction dataset describes a combined 54-country eVisa-or-arrival group but does not assign the extra country to either route, while other paths depend on third-country visas or GCC residence; all 140 residual rows remain unresolved. Oman's current Foreign Ministry second group remains conditional on third-country documents or GCC profession, and its ROP wizard does not establish an exhaustive unconditional route; all 114 residual rows remain held, with Taiwan additionally flagged because the two current authorities disagree on its exemption.

North Macedonia's live MFA selector and entry page continue to time out, while accessible official material proves only EU/Schengen nationality waivers and conditional 2026 third-country-document substitutes. A 2024 Government programme still described the Article 38(6) visa-regime regulation as a proposal, and the live 2026 Official Gazette index through 20 August contains no matching enacted regulation title. The operative current nationality regulation was not recovered, so all 166 residual rows remain held.

Kyrgyzstan's live portal proves genuine eVisa issuance for an eligible applicant, but all 120 residual rows remain unverified. A session-aware retry recovered the nationality backend, yet strong review found that the downstream product endpoint returns a tourist product even when the preceding eligibility gate does not expose Tourist (TS); the apparent 118-passport expansion was rejected before promotion. A later fresh-session pass confirmed `getList7` as the controlling gate, but the host failed before returning gate results for the sampled residual passports and Kosovo and Taiwan were absent from the selector. No product response was promoted without a positive gate. Jordan's current Interior service proves a printable paid eVisa issued before arrival, but nationality eligibility is account-gated and its arrival routes depend on residence or third-country documents; all 144 residual rows remain held. A Ministry of Tourism page last updated 19 August 2026 confirms arrival-fee guidance while expressly warning that some nationalities need a visa before travel, so it cannot supply the missing partition. Bangladesh's current Immigration and Foreign Affairs material provides positive arrival guidance and a general valid-visa requirement, but no exhaustive ordinary-passport schedule; its online MRV is a form lodged at a mission, not eVisa issuance, so all 168 residual rows remain unresolved.

Panama's refreshed complete-partition candidate retracts 36 tentative research mappings that had never been promoted: 33 passports derived from the 2012 Tourist Card waiver plus historical China, Haiti and Kosovo measures. Current Migration and Foreign Ministry pages do not publish an operative 2026 nationality schedule or amendment crosswalk, while third-country visa and residence substitutes are conditional. The 25 independently reviewed canonical Panama rows remain supported; the other 173 foreign rows and the home cell remain unresolved, and no current fallback label is asserted incorrect from the historical decrees alone.

Comoros law requires foreign nationals to hold a visa, preserves unnamed treaty and special-law exceptions, and authorizes border issuance expressly for five-day transit visas only. With no current tourist nationality schedule, all 183 residual foreign rows—including China's unverified fallback waiver—remain held; the queue was corrected after cross-review found China missing from its exact scope. Liberia's current Immigration service proves a conditional, preapproved airport-sticker workflow and requires embassy filing where a Liberian mission exists, but supplies no nationality schedule or exhaustive complement; all 177 residual rows remain unresolved.

Guinea-Bissau retains 22 independently supported foreign relationships and 176 foreign holds, plus an unresolved home cell. No authenticated current government schedule establishes an exhaustive eVisa, arrival-visa or advance-visa residual. Current foreign-government guidance conflicts materially: Germany describes airport issuance, Japan requires advance issuance, and France makes airport issuance conditional on prior Immigration notice; a former electronic application route is reported non-operational. The complete-partition candidate now carries forward the 22 canonical policies rather than mislabeling them unresolved.

The Central African Republic complete-partition audit carries forward its 11 supported foreign rows—five CEMAC waivers, Rwanda's waiver and five independently reviewed advance-visa routes—while preserving 187 foreign holds and the unresolved home cell. No current Central African authority recovered in this pass establishes universal arrival access, electronic issuance or an exhaustive advance-visa complement. Côte d'Ivoire likewise retains only its established cohorts: SNEDAI confirms that online pre-enrolment ends with biometric sticker issuance at Abidjan, normalized as visa on arrival, but publishes no current residual nationality scope. Current Foreign Ministry guidance describes that route only “under conditions,” and an official embassy references the 1993 schedule instruments without reproducing their annex; 155 foreign rows remain unsupported.

The Palestinian Territory remains a complete hold in the destination-side audit. Türkiye's Foreign Ministry says Palestine itself applies no visa to Turkish citizens, but an Israeli visa is required on the Israel-controlled route and a separate Egypt-to-Gaza exception exists. Strong review rejected an apparent `TR → PS` advance-visa correction because that third-country, route-specific condition cannot define a destination-wide Palestinian baseline. Current crossing, COGAT and Palestinian permit material likewise does not yield a general nationality partition.

Tunisia's normalized complete-partition candidate carries forward 24 supported foreign relationships and adds a directly supported citizenship cell, while leaving exactly 174 foreign rows unresolved. Current e-Consulat material names no visa-subject nationality cohort, no current bilateral-waiver register was recovered, and the announced eVisa remains a project rather than an operational issuance route. A 2025 Ministry of Communication Technologies procurement result marks the eVisa-platform tender unsuccessful, providing further evidence against treating the project as a live traveller service. No historical tourism list is treated as a current ordinary-passport schedule.

Mali's refreshed complete-partition candidate carries forward 21 supported relationships and leaves 177 foreign passports unresolved. The live Foreign Ministry assistant depends on nationality, passport type, residence and filing location, and some of its results conflict with the signed AES declaration; neither it nor the available official sources establishes a residual eVisa, visa-on-arrival or advance-visa complement. São Tomé and Príncipe's residual audit likewise adds no row: the official Diário da República text of Law 5/2015 confirms that further exemptions require a decree, while the generic Schengen/United States document exception remains conditional. Switzerland's 15-day and South Africa's 90-day waivers were already supported; Ghana and Rwanda cooperation summaries and the eVisa portal do not establish the remaining 140 passports' unconditional nationality baseline.

The public `/status` matrix contains 38,517 fresh and 6,457 not-covered rows, with zero old or stale rows as of 23 August 2026. Regional coverage is Asia 5,304 of 6,336 (83.7%), Europe 9,967 of 10,299 (96.8%), Africa 8,383 of 11,289 (74.3%), Oceania 3,868 of 4,165 (92.9%), the Caribbean 4,504 of 4,962 (90.8%), the Americas 4,690 of 4,953 (94.7%) and the Middle East 1,801 of 2,970 (60.6%). Canonical evidence contains 404 reviewed batches, 1,966 official sources and 1,306 policies.

The United States adds Trinidad and Tobago `visa_free` for visits of up to 90 days. Belgium adds South Sudan `evisa`; Belgian nationality-specific online-application guidance combines with South Sudan's government pre-travel PDF issuance service. The Netherlands adds South Sudan `evisa` on the same two-part basis and corrects Djibouti from `visa_on_arrival` to `evisa` because Djibouti says its emailed eVisa replaced point-of-entry issuance. Dutch travel guidance also corrects Iran from `visa_on_arrival` to `visa_required` by directing Dutch-passport applicants to Iran's Embassy in The Hague before travel.

United States restrictions for Burkina Faso, Chad and Mali remain conflicts rather than ordinary access rows because current visa-issuance suspensions, limited exceptions and lack of routine issuance cannot be represented by the six-status taxonomy. Canada retains all nine final gaps unresolved. The remaining Belgian and Dutch pairs likewise stay held where official traveler guidance proves that a visa is needed but does not establish whether actual issuance is electronic, at the border or at a mission.

The public `/status` matrix contains 39,203 fresh and 5,771 not-covered rows, with zero old or stale rows as of 24 August 2026. Canonical evidence now contains 644 reviewed batches, 2,819 official sources and 1,802 policies. Africa coverage increases from 8,496 to 8,497 of 11,289 relationships, Asia increases from 5,338 to 5,339 of 6,336 and the Caribbean increases from 4,615 to 4,617 of 4,962; every other regional total is unchanged.

Maldivian ordinary-passport visitors to Cambodia are now directly supported as `visa_free` for up to 30 days. The Maldivian Foreign Ministry says the bilateral exemption took effect on 4 May 2023 and expressly covers ordinary passports; its current visa-free country page independently continues to list Cambodia. Longer stays and work, study or business remain outside the short-visitor policy. Nicaragua-to-Tanzania remains unresolved because the current Tanzanian guidance does not directly map Nicaragua and omission from a named-nationality list is not used as a result.

Barbadian ordinary-passport visitors to Equatorial Guinea are now directly supported as `visa_free`. Barbados's live Foreign Affairs page links its current outbound schedule, whose Equatorial Guinea row says no visa is required and whose agreement note covers all passport categories. Tunisia-to-Equatorial Guinea remains unresolved: the live eVisa service permits applications but does not establish Tunisia's baseline visa requirement, disprove a waiver or cleanly resolve issuance timing.

Bolivia-to-The Bahamas is now directly supported as `visa_free` for a three-month visitor stay. DR Congo-to-The Bahamas is corrected from fallback `evisa` to `visa_required`: current Bahamian regulations expressly designate the Democratic Republic of the Congo and define issuance as a consular endorsement placed in the passport, while the March 2026 online service documentation establishes application filing rather than electronic issuance. South Sudan remains unresolved because neither omission from the visitor table nor omission from the statutory schedule is treated as a complete residual rule.

Uruguay, Indonesia and Cuba to Guyana all remain unresolved after direct official-source comparison. Guyana's Foreign Ministry, Immigration Support Services and Trinidad mission publish mutually incompatible ordinary-passport scope for these three nationalities. No authority supplies a current reconciliation, so the three fallback `visa_free` values remain unverified rather than being promoted through source preference, omission or complement inference.

## Pass 249 result: Paraguay duration correction and conservative Bolivia/Afghanistan replay

Three previously promoted residual audits were replayed against live official sources. They add no new relationship, so complete-matrix evidence remains 39,203 of 44,974 relationships (87.2%), with 5,771 not-covered rows and no old or stale rows as of 24 August 2026. Canonical totals remain 644 reviewed batches, 2,819 official sources and 1,802 policies.

Paraguay's 15 supported residual advance-visa rows remain correct. Its Foreign Ministry expressly says a passport outside the published no-visa list requires a visa, and the current workflow ends with embassy or consulate collection after digital filing, review and interview. The audit corrects one evidence detail: the live Migration Directorate now gives Mongolia a 30-day waiver, not the 90 days stated by the previously retained PDF. Mongolia remains unresolved because that direct waiver still conflicts with the Foreign Ministry's equally current closed residual rule; the correction changes the recorded duration, not the access status.

All 20 scoped Bolivia rows remain uncovered. Bolivia's live Foreign Ministry framework defines Groups I, II and III but does not assign these passports to a group: some API records are null, some are absent, Kosovo appears only inside obsolete Serbia wording and Libya has inconsistent legacy/current labels. Neither a visa portal, a blank group, omission nor another document category is treated as a nationality result.

Germany-to-Afghanistan also remains uncovered. A first review found an undated page under an apparent Afghan Foreign Ministry subdomain claiming that the Bonn consulate issues in-person visas, but independent authentication rejected it: the ministry apex currently serves unrelated gambling content, the page mixes Islamic Republic and Islamic Emirate identities, and Germany's Foreign Office directory disclaims the accuracy of mission contact details. The compromised source was removed rather than promoted. The remaining German directory entry records only that a Bonn contact is listed; it does not establish a current usable visitor-visa issuance route.

## Pass 250 result: Peru and El Salvador exact passport routes

Two exact and independently cross-reviewed relationships are added, raising complete-matrix evidence from 39,203 to 39,205 of 44,974 relationships (87.2%). The public `/status` matrix contains 39,205 fresh and 5,769 not-covered rows, with zero old or stale rows as of 24 August 2026. Canonical evidence now contains 646 reviewed batches, 2,823 official sources and 1,804 policies. Americas coverage increases from 4,827 to 4,829 of 4,953 relationships; every other regional total is unchanged. Both verified statuses already match fallback, so this pass makes no access-status correction.

Indian ordinary-passport visitors to Peru are now directly supported as `visa_required`. Peru's July 2026 ordinary-passport table marks India `SI/NO`: a visa is required by default, but the requirement is waived for travelers who meet the table's qualifying United States, Canada, United Kingdom, Australia or Schengen visa or permanent-residence condition. Peru's current Embassy-in-India procedure confirms advance in-person processing in New Delhi, a normal 7-to-10-working-day period after interview and multiple-entry tourist-visa validity of up to 183 days. The conditional document route remains an exception rather than being flattened into passport-wide visa-free access.

Macao SAR ordinary-passport visitors to El Salvador are now directly supported as `visa_required`. Macao's live passport-authority API maps its ordinary passport to El Salvador's official Special Entry Permit service and marks neither visa-free nor visa-on-arrival access. Salvadoran Immigration requires a favorable PEI decision before travel and treats the resulting passport-stamped permit as equivalent to an entry visa. Independent review therefore rejected an initial arrival-visa interpretation: border stamping after mandatory advance approval does not make this a walk-up visa on arrival, ETA or electronically issued visa.

Saint Kitts and Nevis-to-Kenya was replayed but remains the existing unresolved hold. Kenya's controlling regulation and live eTA page both publish the malformed name `St. Kitts and Navis`, and no current official source links that label to the KN ordinary-passport issuer. The repeated typo is not silently repaired into a visa-free classification.

## Pass 253 result: Ecuador agreement, Tanzania statute and Kosovo Gazette check

HKSAR ordinary-passport visitors to Ecuador are now directly supported as `visa_free` for up to 90 days. The reciprocal Visa Abolition Agreement took effect on 3 October 1999 and expressly grants visa-free Ecuador visits to HKSAR passport holders; Hong Kong Immigration's current destination selector continues to list Ecuador with the same 90-day duration. No official termination or contrary current rule was recovered. Macao-to-Ecuador remains unresolved because neither Ecuador's current PRC nationality entry nor the dynamic Macao travel-convenience service directly maps an ordinary Macao SAR passport; treatment is not transferred from China or Hong Kong.

Nicaraguan ordinary-passport visitors to Tanzania are now directly supported as `visa_on_arrival`. Tanzania's operative regulation expressly places a country outside the complete visa-free Third Schedule in the visa-without-prior-approval class; the 2025 amendment replaces that schedule in full and does not list Nicaragua. Nicaragua is also outside the complete referral schedule. The statute permits issuance at designated entry points and current Immigration guidance confirms arrival issuance at an official entry point. This is an express statutory partition, not an inference from a portal or non-exhaustive web-list omission.

Fiji-to-Kosovo remains unresolved. Signed Decision No. 14/280 places Fiji under a visa regime but expressly makes effectiveness depend on publication in Kosovo's Official Gazette. The live MFA short-stay page still names Fiji as visa-free, while direct current Gazette searches did not retrieve the required publication or a later controlling act. Failure to find a Gazette record is not treated as proof of nonpublication, and the conflicting official material is not flattened into either `visa_free` or `visa_required`.

Liberia's live Immigration Service route is a pre-approved visa-on-arrival process, not electronic issuance: it requires the traveller to use a Liberian embassy when one exists in the traveller's country and ends with a physical visa sticker at Roberts International Airport. The Tourism Authority calls the service an eVisa while warning travellers to reconfirm requirements, but that looser terminology does not override the Immigration Service's operational description. The 141 fallback `evisa` rows and 22 fallback `visa_required` rows are therefore unverified and likely flatten a mission-availability split; without a nationality and embassy-coverage schedule, neither cohort can be safely relabelled.

Tunisia's current E-Consulat page says unnamed visa-subject nationalities must contact the competent Tunisian consulate and allows at least 20 days from filing, but it publishes no nationality schedule. A 2024 Presidency page describes an eVisa platform in future terms, and the Communication Technologies Ministry's 2025 procurement result records the platform tender as unsuccessful. Those materials establish neither a live electronic route nor an exhaustive required-visa or waiver complement. All 59 fallback `visa_free` rows and 94 fallback `visa_required` rows in this residual remain pending.

Trinidad and Tobago's current Homeland Security PDF is an affirmative waiver list only and states no legal complement for omitted countries.
